Reduced-Rank Autoregressive Model for High-Dimensional Multivariate Network Time Series
Qi Lyu, Xiaoyu Zhang, Guodong Li, Di Wang † \dagger Shanghai Jiao Tong University, ‡ \ddagger Tongji University, § \lx@sectionsign University of Hong Kong

Abstract
Multivariate network time series are ubiquitous in modern systems, yet existing network autoregressive models typically treat nodes as scalar processes, ignoring cross-variable spillovers. To capture these complex interactions without the curse of dimensionality, we propose the Reduced-Rank Network Autoregressive (RRNAR) model. Our framework introduces a separable bilinear transition structure that couples the known network topology with a learnable low-rank variable subspace. We estimate the model using a novel Scaled Gradient Descent (ScaledGD) algorithm, explicitly designed to bridge the gap between rigid network scalars and flexible factor components. Theoretically, we establish non-asymptotic error bounds under a novel distance metric. A key finding is a network-induced blessing of dimensionality: for sparse networks, the estimation accuracy for network parameters improves as the network size grows. Applications to traffic and server monitoring networks demonstrate that RRNAR significantly outperforms univariate and unstructured benchmarks by identifying latent cross-channel propagation mechanisms.



Keywords
: Matrix-valued time series, network model, autoregression, dimension reduction.

1
Introduction

1.1
Background and Motivation

In the analysis of modern interconnected systems, including infrastructure sensor networks
(
Hsu et al., 2021
)
, global financial markets
(
Barigozzi et al., 2024
)
, and multi-channel social platforms
(
Högberg, 2023
)
, data increasingly emerges in the form of multivariate network time series. Consider a large network with
N
nodes, where for each node, one observes a
D
-dimensional vector of variables evolving over time. Modeling such data requires capturing a dual dependency structure: the internal dynamic interactions among the multiple variables within each node, and the external spillovers transmitted across the network topology.

While the literature on network time series has matured, it has remained predominantly univariate. The standard network autoregressive (NAR) model
(
Zhu et al., 2017
)
and its variants
(
Zhu et al., 2019
;
Zhu and Pan, 2020
)
typically treat nodes as scalar processes (effectively
D=1
). This approach implies a restrictive assumption that network spillovers are strictly
channel-specific
. In other words, variable
k
at a neighboring node is assumed to affect only variable
k
at the focal node. In reality, however, network propagation is frequently
cross-variable
. For instance, in a traffic sensor network, a surge in occupancy at an upstream node is often the physical precursor to a drop in speed at downstream locations. By treating these series individually, univariate models fail to identify the cross-channel mechanisms. This limitation suggests that standard estimators are not merely less efficient, but potentially suffer from omitted variable bias regarding the true nature of network diffusion.

The challenge lies in modeling these cross-variable linkages without succumbing to the curse of dimensionality. A naive solution involves stacking all variables into a massive vector autoregression (VAR), but this requires estimating
\mathcal{O}(N^{2}D^{2})
parameters, which is computationally infeasible and statistical inefficient when
N
and
D
are large. Conversely, applying matrix autoregression
(
Chen et al., 2021
, MAR,)
or Reduced-Rank MAR
(
Xiao et al., 2022
)
effectively handles the multivariate dimension but typically ignores the observable network topology. These methods treat cross-node dependence as a latent factor to be learned entirely from data. While flexible, this approach discards valuable domain knowledge provided by the known topology. This omission can lead to identification issues and statistical inefficiency, particularly when the network structure is sparse and rigid.

1.2
Contributions

This article makes three primary contributions. First, we propose the Reduced-Rank Network AutoRegressive (RRNAR) model, a novel framework that bridges the gap between univariate network models and high-dimensional matrix autoregressions. We express the multivariate network time series data as a matrix
\mathbf{Y}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times D}
and formulate the transition dynamics via a separable bilinear structure:
\mathbf{Y}_{t}=\mathbf{B}_{\textup{net}}\mathbf{Y}_{t-1}\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}}^{\top}+\mathbf{E}_{t}
.
The innovation lies in the asymmetric constraints imposed on the operators. The network operator
\mathbf{B}_{\textup{net}}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
is structured by the observable graph topology (
\beta_{\textup{A}}\mathbf{I}_{N}+\beta_{\textup{N}}\mathbf{W}_{N}
), while the variable operator
\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}}\in\mathbb{R}^{D\times D}
is low-rank with rank
r
. This formulation provides a rigorous structural interpretation: it posits that network spillovers do not occur between every pair of raw variables, but rather through a low-dimensional subspace of latent factors, allowing us to capture complex cross-variable propagation mechanisms while avoiding the curse of dimensionality.

Second, we address the unique optimization challenges posed by this
scalar-plus-subspace
landscape. The model parameters comprise two distinct blocks: rigid network scalars and a rotationally invariant variable subspace. This structural mismatch induces a pathological loss landscape where gradients with respect to the two blocks exhibit vastly different scales, rendering standard gradient descent ineffective. We develop a Scaled Gradient Descent (ScaledGD) algorithm with block-specific preconditioners. These preconditioners explicitly balance the interaction weights of the network and variable components, ensuring linear computational convergence to the global optimum despite the non-convexity.

Third, we establish the non-asymptotic properties of the estimator under an equivalence-invariant distance metric. Our theoretical analysis reveals a blessing of dimensionality phenomenon specific to the network parameters. We show that while the estimation error for the variable subspace scales with the effective dimension
\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{Dr/T})
, the errors for the autoregressive effect
\beta_{\textup{A}}
and network spillover parameter
\beta_{\textup{N}}
scale with
\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{Dr/NT})
and
\mathcal{O}(\|\mathbf{W}_{N}\|_{\textup{F}}^{-1}\sqrt{Dr/T})
, respectively. For sparse networks, where
\|\mathbf{W}_{N}\|_{\textup{F}}
grows with
N
, this implies that estimation accuracy improves as the network size increases. This result formally distinguishes our method from standard matrix autoregressions, demonstrating that the rigid network structure acts as a powerful variance reduction device that decouples the network estimation rate from the high-dimensional variable space.

1.3
Related Literature

Our work sits at the intersection of network statistics, high-dimensional time series, and non-convex optimization, distinct from existing approaches in its handling of parameter heterogeneity.

First, we contribute to the literature on network autoregression (NAR). Seminal works by
Zhu et al., 2017
established the framework for analyzing time series distributed across a graph, using the adjacency matrix to mitigate the curse of dimensionality. Subsequent research extended this to account for node heterogeneity
(
Zhu and Pan, 2020
)
and community structures
(
Chen et al., 2023
)
. As the dimension of nodal attributes (
D
) increases, recent works such as the group matrix NAR
(
Ren et al., 2022
)
have attempted to model multi-dimensional interactions. However, a significant limitation of these extensions is their reliance on a known column network or an explicit graph among variables. In many scenarios (e.g., corporate financial statements), the network among variables is latent or unknown. Our RRNAR model avoids this misspecification risk by assuming a low-rank structure for the variable domain rather than a pre-specified graph.

Second, our work relates to the rapidly growing field of matrix autoregressions
(
Chen et al., 2021
;
Xiao et al., 2022
)
. These models typically utilize bilinear forms
\mathbf{Y}_{t}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{Y}_{t-1}\mathbf{B}^{\top}+\mathbf{E}_{t}
where both
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
are treated as unknown low-rank matrices to be estimated from data. In contrast, our framework is structurally hybrid: the left operator is constrained by the physical graph (
\beta_{\textup{A}}\mathbf{I}_{N}+\beta_{\textup{N}}\mathbf{W}_{N}
), while the right operator is a learnable low-rank subspace. This formulation transforms the problem from pure matrix recovery to a parameter-plus-subspace estimation problem. This allows us to leverage domain knowledge from the observed network, which
Xiao et al., 2022
and similar purely data-driven methods discard. Closely related is
Hsu et al., 2021
, which also proposes a spatio-temporal matrix model; however, their approach relies on element-wise sparsity, which is less effective than our factor-based approach for capturing pervasive dense correlations in high-dimensional systems.

Third, regarding spatial econometrics, our work generalizes dynamic spatial panel data models
(
Yu et al., 2008
)
to the high-dimensional multivariate setting. While recent studies have addressed diverging
N

(
Zhu et al., 2020
)
or diverging
D
via factor models
(
Shi et al., 2025
)
, they typically lack the capacity to characterize the coupling between temporal dynamics and spatial spillovers. Most closely related to our setting is the recent work by
Wu et al., 2025
, which also analyzes matrix-valued data incorporating auxiliary network information. However, their framework differs fundamentally in objective: it emphasizes the simultaneous identification of contemporaneous network spillovers versus lagged autoregressive effects. In contrast, our RRNAR model focuses on utilizing the network structure to achieve dimension reduction within the lag operator itself. This allows us to specifically address the regime of doubly diverging dimensions (
N,D\to\infty
) via the low-rank variable approximation, a feature distinct from the identification strategy in
Wu et al., 2025
.

Finally, in terms of optimization, we extend the theory of Scaled Gradient Descent
(
Tong et al., 2021
)
to asymmetric landscapes. While existing theory covers symmetric low-rank problems like matrix completion, our objective function mixes rigid scalar parameters with a free subspace. We show that block-specific preconditioning effectively bridges the geometric gap between these structured and unstructured components, ensuring global convergence.

1.4
Notations and Outline

For a vector
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
,
\|\mathbf{x}\|_{2}
denotes the Euclidean norm. For a matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
, we denote its transpose, Frobenius norm, spectral norm, and the
r
-th largest singular value by
\mathbf{A}^{\top}
,
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\mathrm{F}}
,
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}
, and
\sigma_{r}(\mathbf{A})
, respectively. The vectorization operator
\text{vec}(\mathbf{A})
stacks the columns of
\mathbf{A}
into a vector of length
mn
, and the inverse operation is denoted by
\text{mat}(\cdot)
. The Kronecker product is denoted by
\otimes
. For a square matrix
\mathbf{A}
,
\rho(\mathbf{A})
denotes the spectral radius and
\text{tr}(\mathbf{A})
denotes the trace. We denote the set of
r\times r
invertible matrices by
\mathrm{GL}(r)
. The identity matrix of dimension
N
is denoted by
\mathbf{I}_{N}
. Throughout the article,
C
denotes a generic positive constant whose value may vary from line to line. The notation
a_{n}\lesssim b_{n}
indicates that
a_{n}\leq Cb_{n}
for some constant
C
independent of the sample size and dimensions.

The remainder of the article is organized as follows. Section
2
introduces the RRNAR model, and discusses its interpretations and identification conditions. Section
3
details the estimation methodology and rank selection procedure. Section
4
provides the theoretical analysis, establishing computational convergence guarantees and non-asymptotic statistical error bounds. Section
5
presents simulation results and Section
6
applies the proposed methodology to two real data examples. Section
7
concludes the article with some short discussions. Technical proofs and additional details are relegated to Supplementary Materials.

2
Model

2.1
Multivariate Network Time Series

Consider a panel of multivariate time series indexed by nodes
i=1,\dots,N
in a directed network and by time periods
t=1,\dots,T
. At each node
i
and time
t
, we observe a
D
-dimensional vector
\mathbf{y}_{it}=(Y_{i1t},\dots,Y_{iDt})^{\top}
. We stack these observations to form the
N\times D
matrix
\mathbf{Y}_{t}=(Y_{ijt})_{1\leq i\leq N,1\leq j\leq D}
, where rows correspond to nodes and columns to variables. This data structure is pertinent to various economic applications, such as networks of firms characterized by multiple financial ratios, trade networks with section-specific flows, or digital platforms tracking multiple user activity metrics.

Let
\mathbf{N}=(n_{ij})\in\{0,1\}^{N\times N}
denote the adjacency matrix, where
n_{ij}=1
indicates a directed edge from node
i
to node
j
, and zero otherwise. We assume no self-loops (
n_{ii}=0
). Let
n_{i}=\sum_{j\neq i}n_{ij}
denote the out-degree of node
i
, and define the row-normalized weight matrix
\mathbf{W}_{N}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
as

\mathbf{W}_{N}=\text{diag}(n_{1}^{-1},\dots,n_{N}^{-1})\mathbf{N}.

(1)

Row normalization ensures that each row of
\mathbf{W}_{N}
sums to one, allowing the spatial lag to be interpreted as a weighted average of neighbors’ characteristics. If the network is undirected,
\mathbf{N}
is symmetric.

Data of this nature typically exhibit dependence along three dimensions: (i) temporal persistence within variables; (ii) cross-sectional dependence across nodes induced by network linkages; and (iii) contemporaneous and dynamic covariation among the
D
variables within nodes. A viable statistical model must jointly capture these dependencies while mitigating the curse of dimensionality inherent when
N
and
D
are large.

2.2
Reduced-Rank Network Autoregressive Model

To model these joint dynamics, we propose a matrix autoregressive specification with a separable bilinear structure. We model the evolution of the state matrix
\mathbf{Y}_{t}
as:

\mathbf{Y}_{t}=\mathbf{B}_{\textup{net}}\mathbf{Y}_{t-1}\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}}^{\top}+\mathbf{E}_{t},

(2)

where
\mathbf{B}_{\textup{net}}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
governs cross-nodal spillovers,
\mathbf{B}_{\mathrm{var}}\in\mathbb{R}^{D\times D}
governs cross-variable dynamics, and
\mathbf{E}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times D}
is a matrix white-noise process with mean zero.

The core innovation of our framework lies in the
asymmetric structural constraints
imposed on the left and right operators to reflect the physical reality of the system. To incorporate the network topology parsimoniously, we parametrize the node operator as a linear combination of the identity matrix and the network weight matrix:

\mathbf{B}_{\mathrm{net}}=\beta_{\mathrm{A}}\mathbf{I}_{N}+\beta_{\mathrm{N}}\mathbf{W}_{N},{}

(3)

where
\beta_{\textup{A}}
captures the autoregressive persistence of the node itself, and
\beta_{\textup{N}}\in\mathbb{R}
captures the network spillover effect. This reduces the
\mathcal{O}(N^{2})
spatial parameter space to two scalars.

To achieve parsimony in the variable dimension when
D
is large, we impose a reduced-rank structure on
\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}}
. Specifically, we assume that
\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}}
has rank
r\ll D
, admitting the factorization

\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}}=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{V}^{\top},\quad\mathbf{U},\mathbf{V}\in\mathbb{R}^{D\times r},\quad r\ll D.

(4)

This specification implies that the cross-variable dynamics are driven by
r
latent factors. Substituting (
3
) and (
4
) into (
2
) yields the Reduced-Rank Network Autoregressive (RRNAR) model:

\mathbf{Y}_{t}=(\beta_{\textup{A}}\mathbf{I}_{N}+\beta_{\textup{N}}\mathbf{W}_{N})\mathbf{Y}_{t-1}\mathbf{V}\mathbf{U}^{\top}+\mathbf{E}_{t}.

(5)

Remark 1
.

The RRNAR model nests several standard specifications. If
N=1
, (
5
) reduces to a reduced-rank VAR (RRVAR) model for multivariate time series
(
Reinsel and Velu, 1998
)
. If
D=1
, it simplifies to a univariate Network Autoregressive (NAR) model
(
Zhu et al., 2017
)
. The proposed framework generalizes these approaches by allowing interactions between the low-rank variable structure and the network topology.

Remark 2
.

The scalar parameterization in (
3
) imposes homogeneous autoregressive and network effects across nodes. While this restriction significantly improves estimation efficiency in high-dimensional settings, it may be relaxed to accommodate group-specific heterogeneity or other flexible structures, as discussed in Section
7
.

Remark 3
.

The specification extends naturally to the lag-
L
dynamics. Due to space limit, we focus on the lag-1 RRNAR model in the main article, and relegate the lag-
L
model, and corresponding estimation methods and theory in Appendix
E
of Supplementary Materials.

2.3
Model Interpretations

The separable bilinear structure of the RRNAR model is not merely a computational device for dimension reduction; it encodes a specific structural hypothesis about the data generating process. To make the mechanics of the model concrete, we consider a
running example
of a traffic sensor network throughout this section. In this setting, nodes are sensors on a highway, the network
\mathbf{W}_{N}
represents road connectivity (upstream/downstream), and the variables
\mathbf{y}_{it}
include flow, occupancy (density), and speed. We formally analyze this dataset and validate these interpretations in Section
6
.

2.3.1
Network-Interacted Latent Factor Process

The first interpretation views the system as a dynamic factor model where the factors themselves, instead of the raw variables, are the objects subject to network diffusion. Let
\mathbf{F}_{t-1}=\mathbf{Y}_{t-1}\mathbf{V}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times r}
denote the matrix of latent factor scores at time
t-1
. The conditional expectation
\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{Y}_{t}|\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]
can be decomposed into a three-stage mechanism:

1.

Dimension Reduction:
The high-dimensional observation
\mathbf{Y}_{t-1}
is projected onto the subspace spanned by
\mathbf{V}
(
\mathbf{F}_{t-1}=\mathbf{Y}_{t-1}\mathbf{V}
). This aggregates the
D
raw variables into
r
fundamental factors. In our running example, these factors might represent abstract states like “congestion level” or “throughput capacity”, which are not directly observed but are linear combinations of the raw speed, flow, and occupancy measurements.

2.

Network Propagation:
These latent factors propagate across the physical graph according to the parametric node operator:

\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}=(\beta_{\textup{A}}\mathbf{I}_{N}+\beta_{\textup{N}}\mathbf{W}_{N})\mathbf{F}_{t-1}.

(6)

This equation dictates the dynamics. The term
\beta_{\textup{A}}\mathbf{F}_{t-1}
captures the persistence of the factors within the node (e.g., congestion persisting at a specific location). The term
\beta_{\textup{N}}\mathbf{W}_{N}\mathbf{F}_{t-1}
captures the spatial spillover (e.g., congestion diffusing from upstream neighbors). Crucially, this implies that only the systematic variation captured by the factors is transmitted across the network, acting as a powerful denoising mechanism.

3.

Reconstruction:
The propagated factors
\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}
are mapped back to the original variable space via the loading matrix
\mathbf{U}
:

\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{Y}_{t}|\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]=\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}\mathbf{U}^{\top}.

(7)

This final step determines how the abstract factors manifest in the observed variables at time
t
.

2.3.2
Decomposition of Cross-Channel Spillovers

The second interpretation focuses on the structure of the transition coefficients. The model implies that the coefficient linking variable
q
at node
j
(at time
t-1
) to variable
k
at node
i
(at time
t
) is not a free parameter, but is strictly factorized. Specifically, the scalar coefficient
a_{ij,kq}
is determined by the product of two independent components:

a_{ij,kq}=\begin{cases}\beta_{\textup{A}}\times(\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{q}),&\text{if }i=j\quad\text{(internal dynamics)},\\
\beta_{\textup{N}}w_{ij}\times(\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{q}),&\text{if }i\neq j\quad\text{(network spillovers)}.\end{cases}

(8)

This factorization reveals the strength of any spillover is driven by two distinct mechanisms:

1.

Network Proximity
(
\beta_{\textup{N}}w_{ij}
): This term dictates
where
influence can flow. It is non-zero only if node
j
is a physically connected neighbor of node
i
, ensuring that propagation respects the known graph topology.

2.

Variable Coupling
(
\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{q}
): This term dictates
what
information is transmitted. It captures the functional relationship between the source variable
q
and the target variable
k
. The coefficient is non-zero only if the variables share a common orientation in the latent factor space.

This structure highlights the key advantage of our framework over univariate NAR models. A univariate model forces
a_{ij,kq}=0
whenever
k\neq q
, effectively assuming that the variable coupling matrix is diagonal (i.e., variables are isolated). In contrast, RRNAR allows for
cross-channel spillovers
, such as upstream occupancy affecting downstream speed, provided that the variables are mechanically linked through the factor space (
\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{q}\neq 0
). This allows the model to capture complex physical interactions while using the network structure to regularize the estimation.

2.4
Stationarity and Identification

The model in (
5
) admits a vector autoregressive representation:

\text{vec}(\mathbf{Y}_{t})=(\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}}\otimes\mathbf{B}_{\textup{net}})\text{vec}(\mathbf{Y}_{t-1})+\text{vec}(\mathbf{E}_{t})=:\mathbf{A}\text{vec}(\mathbf{Y}_{t-1})+\text{vec}(\mathbf{E}_{t}).

(9)

Weak stationarity requires that the spectral radius
\rho(\mathbf{A})<1
. Since
\rho(\mathbf{A})=\rho(\mathbf{B}_{\textup{net}})\rho(\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}})
and
\rho(\mathbf{W}_{N})=1
due to row-normalization, we have
\rho(\mathbf{B}_{\textup{net}})\leq|\beta_{\textup{A}}|+|\beta_{\textup{N}}|
. Thus, a sufficient condition for stationarity is
(|\beta_{\textup{A}}|+|\beta_{\textup{N}}|)\cdot\rho(\mathbf{B}_{\text{var}})<1
.

The bilinear reduced-rank structure introduces two forms of identification ambiguity. First, the pair
(\mathbf{B}_{\textup{net}},\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}})
and
(c\mathbf{B}_{\textup{net}},c^{-1}\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}})
produce the same
\mathbf{A}
for any nonzero
c
. Second, the factorization
\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}}=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{V}^{\top}
is invariant to the transformation
(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{V})\mapsto(\mathbf{U}\mathbf{Q},\mathbf{V}\mathbf{Q}^{-\top})
for any invertible
\mathbf{Q}\in\text{GL}(r)
. However, the Kronecker product
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}}\otimes\mathbf{B}_{\textup{net}}
is uniquely identified, and hence we treat
\mathbf{A}
as the identified object.

Let
\mathbf{\Theta}=(\beta_{\textup{A}},\beta_{\textup{N}},\mathbf{U},\mathbf{V})
represent the tuple of parameters, and denote
\mathbf{\Theta}^{*}=(\beta_{\textup{A}}^{*},\beta_{\textup{N}}^{*},\mathbf{U}^{*},\mathbf{V}^{*})
as their true values. We define
\mathbf{B}_{\textup{net}}^{*}=\beta_{\textup{A}}^{*}\mathbf{I}_{N}+\beta_{\textup{N}}^{*}\mathbf{W}_{N}
,
\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}}^{*}=\mathbf{U}^{*}{\mathbf{V}^{*}}^{\top}
, and
\mathbf{A}^{*}=\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}}^{*}\otimes\mathbf{B}_{\textup{net}}^{*}
.
To remove the scaling ambiguity, we impose the norm-balancing constraint
\|\mathbf{B}_{\textup{net}}^{*}\|_{\text{F}}=\|\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}}^{*}\|_{\text{F}}
; under this constraint
\mathbf{B}_{\textup{net}}^{*}
and
\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}}^{*}
are identified up to a joint sign change. Furthermore, we take the compact SVD
\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}}^{*}=\mathbf{L}^{*}\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*}{\mathbf{R}^{*}}^{\top}
, and set
\mathbf{U}^{*}:=\mathbf{L}^{*}{\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*}}^{1/2}
and
\mathbf{V}^{*}:=\mathbf{R}^{*}{\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*}}^{1/2}
. For estimation and convergence analysis, we define the equivalence class
\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{\Theta})
containing all parameter tuples generating the same dynamics:

\begin{split}\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{\Theta})=\Big\{(\beta^{\prime}_{\textup{A}},\beta^{\prime}_{\text{N}},\mathbf{U}^{\prime},\mathbf{V}^{\prime})\ \big|\ &\beta^{\prime}_{\textup{A}}=(c_{1}c_{2})^{-1}\beta_{\textup{A}},\beta^{\prime}_{\textup{N}}=(c_{1}c_{2})^{-1}\beta_{\textup{N}},\mathbf{U}^{\prime}=c_{1}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{Q},\ \\
&\mathbf{V}^{\prime}=c_{2}\mathbf{V}\mathbf{Q}^{-\top}\ \text{for some}\ c_{1},c_{2}\neq 0\text{ and }\mathbf{Q}\in\mathrm{GL}(r)\Big\}.\end{split}

(10)

A key theoretical contribution of this article is the construction of a distance metric invariant to this equivalence class. We define the squared distance between
\mathbf{\Theta}
and
\mathbf{\Theta}^{*}
as

\begin{split}&\mathrm{dist}(\mathbf{\Theta},\mathbf{\Theta}^{*})^{2}\\
=&\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{\Theta}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{\Theta})\end{subarray}}\biggl\{\left(\beta_{\textup{A}}^{\prime}-\beta_{\textup{A}}^{*}\right)^{2}\|\mathbf{I}_{N}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\|\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}}^{*}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}+\left(\beta_{\textup{N}}^{\prime}-\beta_{\textup{N}}^{*}\right)^{2}\|\mathbf{W}_{N}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\|\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}}^{*}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad+\|(\mathbf{U}^{\prime}-\mathbf{U}^{*})\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*1/2}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\|\mathbf{B}_{\textup{net}}^{*}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}+\|(\mathbf{V}^{\prime}-\mathbf{V}^{*})\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*1/2}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\|\mathbf{B}_{\textup{net}}^{*}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\biggr\}\\
=&\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{Q}\in\mathrm{GL}(r),\\
c_{1},c_{2}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\biggl\{\left((c_{1}c_{2})^{-1}\beta_{\textup{A}}-\beta_{\textup{A}}^{*}\right)^{2}\|\mathbf{I}_{N}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\|\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}}^{*}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}+\left((c_{1}c_{2})^{-1}\beta_{\textup{N}}-\beta_{\textup{N}}^{*}\right)^{2}\|\mathbf{W}_{N}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\|\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}}^{*}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad+\|(c_{1}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{Q}-\mathbf{U}^{*})\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*1/2}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\|\mathbf{B}_{\textup{net}}^{*}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}+\|(c_{2}\mathbf{V}\mathbf{Q}^{-\top}-\mathbf{V}^{*})\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*1/2}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\|\mathbf{B}_{\textup{net}}^{*}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\biggr\}.\end{split}

(11)

This metric naturally weights the scalar discrepancies by the magnitude of the matrix component and vice-versa, ensuring a balanced evaluation of error. The following proposition establishes that this metric is locally equivalent to the Frobenius error of the identified
\mathbf{A}
.

Proposition 1

(Distance-Error Equivalence)
.

For any
\mathbf{\Theta}
and
\mathbf{\Theta}^{*}
, if the distance
\mathrm{dist}(\mathbf{\Theta},\mathbf{\Theta}^{*})\lesssim\phi\sigma_{r}(\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}}^{*})
, where
\|\mathbf{B}_{\textup{net}}^{*}\|_{\mathrm{F}}=\|\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}}^{*}\|_{\mathrm{F}}=\phi
, then for
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}}\otimes\mathbf{B}_{\textup{net}}
and
\mathbf{A}^{*}=\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}}^{*}\otimes\mathbf{B}_{\textup{net}}^{*}
,

C_{1}\|\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{A}^{*}\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq\mathrm{dist}(\mathbf{\Theta},\mathbf{\Theta}^{*})\leq C_{2}\|\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{A}^{*}\|_{\mathrm{F}},

(12)

where
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are positive universal constants.

3
Estimation Methodology

This section details the estimation strategy for the RRNAR model. We frame the estimation as a non-convex optimization problem and propose a Scaled Gradient Descent (ScaledGD) algorithm designed to navigate the ill-conditioned loss surface induced by the parameter structural heterogeneity.

3.1
Loss Function and Gradient Geometry

Consider a sample
\mathbf{Y}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{Y}_{T+1}
generated by the RRNAR process. Assuming the rank
r
is fixed, the estimator
\widehat{\mathbf{\Theta}}=(\widehat{\beta}_{\textup{A}},\widehat{\beta}_{\textup{N}},\widehat{\mathbf{U}},\widehat{\mathbf{V}})
is obtained by minimizing the Frobenius loss:

\mathcal{L}_{T}(\mathbf{\Theta})=\frac{1}{2T}\sum_{t=2}^{T+1}\left\|\mathbf{Y}_{t}-(\beta_{\textup{A}}\mathbf{I}_{N}+\beta_{\textup{N}}\mathbf{W}_{N})\mathbf{Y}_{t-1}\mathbf{V}\mathbf{U}^{\top}\right\|_{\text{F}}^{2}.

(13)

While the loss is smooth, its optimization landscape is characterized by a severe geometric pathology arising from
structural heterogeneity
. The parameters fall into two distinct classes with fundamentally different scaling properties: the network parameters (
\beta_{\textup{A}},\beta_{\textup{N}}
) are scalars governing rigid, high-norm matrices (
\mathbf{I}_{N},\mathbf{W}_{N}
), while the variable parameters (
\mathbf{U},\mathbf{V}
) define a rotationally invariant subspace.

The bilinear structure
\mathbf{B}_{\textup{net}}\otimes\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}}
introduces a scaling ambiguity: the transformation
(\mathbf{B}_{\textup{net}},\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}})\to(c\mathbf{B}_{\textup{net}},c^{-1}\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}})
leaves the loss invariant but skews the gradients. As
c
increases, the gradient with respect to the network scalars vanishes (
\propto c^{-1}
), while the gradient for the variable matrices explodes (
\propto c
). Standard gradient descent, which applies a single global learning rate (i.e., gradient descent step size), cannot adapt to these disparate scales, leading to oscillation or stalled convergence. For brevity, the details of partial gradients and scaling ambiguities are presented in Appendix
A
of Supplementary Materials.

3.2
Scaled Gradient Descent Algorithm

To address this gradient imbalance, we propose a Scaled Gradient Descent (ScaledGD) algorithm. The core innovation is a block-specific preconditioning step that standardizes the effective curvature of the problem. Denote the partial gradient of
\mathcal{L}_{T}(\mathbf{\Theta})
with respect to the component
\mathbf{M}
as
\nabla_{\mathbf{M}}\mathcal{L}_{T}(\mathbf{\Theta})
. At iteration
j
, the update rules, with a single global
\eta>0
, are defined as follows:

\begin{split}\beta_{\text{A}}^{(j+1)}&\leftarrow\beta_{\text{A}}^{(j)}-\eta\cdot\underbrace{\left(\|\mathbf{B}_{\text{var}}^{(j)}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{-2}\cdot\|\mathbf{I}_{N}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{-2}\right)}_{\text{Scalar Preconditioner}}\cdot\nabla_{\beta_{\text{A}}}\mathcal{L}_{T}(\mathbf{\Theta}^{(j)}),\\
\beta_{\text{N}}^{(j+1)}&\leftarrow\beta_{\text{N}}^{(j)}-\eta\cdot\underbrace{\left(\|\mathbf{B}_{\text{var}}^{(j)}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{-2}\cdot\|\mathbf{W}_{N}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{-2}\right)}_{\text{Scalar Preconditioner}}\cdot\nabla_{\beta_{\text{N}}}\mathcal{L}_{T}(\mathbf{\Theta}^{(j)}),\\
\mathbf{U}^{(j+1)}&\leftarrow\mathbf{U}^{(j)}-\eta\cdot\underbrace{\|\mathbf{B}_{\text{net}}^{(j)}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{-2}}_{\text{Scale}}\nabla_{\mathbf{U}}\mathcal{L}_{T}(\mathbf{\Theta}^{(j)})\underbrace{(\mathbf{V}^{(j)\top}\mathbf{V}^{(j)})^{-1}}_{\text{Curvature}},\\
\mathbf{V}^{(j+1)}&\leftarrow\mathbf{V}^{(j)}-\eta\cdot\underbrace{\|\mathbf{B}_{\text{net}}^{(j)}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{-2}}_{\text{Scale}}\nabla_{\mathbf{V}}\mathcal{L}_{T}(\mathbf{\Theta}^{(j)})\underbrace{(\mathbf{U}^{(j)\top}\mathbf{U}^{(j)})^{-1}}_{\text{Curvature}}.\end{split}

(14)

The rationale for these preconditioners is three-fold:

1.

Scale Invariance:
The terms
\|\mathbf{B}_{\text{var}}\|_{\textup{F}}^{-2}
and
\|\mathbf{B}_{\text{net}}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{-2}
normalize the gradients by the magnitude of the complementary operator. This ensures that the optimization trajectory is invariant to the scaling ambiguity
c
, effectively balancing the step sizes between the left and right operators.

2.

Feature Balancing:
The factors
\|\mathbf{I}_{N}\|_{\text{F}}^{-2}
and
\|\mathbf{W}_{N}\|_{\text{F}}^{-2}
place the autoregressive and network coefficients on a comparable metric. This is particularly important for dense networks where
\|\mathbf{W}_{N}\|_{\text{F}}\ll\|\mathbf{I}_{N}\|_{\text{F}}=\sqrt{N}
, preventing the autoregressive term from dominating the network update.

3.

Subspace Alignment:
The matrix preconditioners
(\mathbf{V}^{\top}\mathbf{V})^{-1}
and
(\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{U})^{-1}
act as quasi-Newton updates within the low-rank latent space. They align the gradient steps with the geometry of the factor manifold, accelerating convergence in directions where the curvature is flat.

The complete procedure is summarized in Algorithm
1
. For computational efficiency, we utilize the fact that
\|\mathbf{B}_{\text{net}}\|_{\text{F}}^{2}=\beta_{\text{A}}^{2}N+\beta_{\text{N}}\|\mathbf{W}_{N}\|_{\text{F}}^{2}
, and replace the full norm calculations with trace operations where possible; e.g.,
\|\mathbf{B}_{\text{var}}\|_{\text{F}}^{2}=\|\mathbf{U}\mathbf{V}^{\top}\|_{\text{F}}^{2}=\text{tr}(\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{V}^{\top}\mathbf{V})
. The algorithm requires the Gram matrices
\mathbf{U}^{(j)\top}\mathbf{U}^{(j)}
and
\mathbf{V}^{(j)\top}\mathbf{V}^{(j)}
to remain nonsingular. Theorem
1
in Section
4.1
guarantees that their invertibility holds throughout the optimization trajectory.

Algorithm 1

ScaledGD Algorithm for RRNAR Model

1:

Input:
data
\{\mathbf{Y}_{t}\}_{t=1}^{T+1}
,
\mathbf{W}_{N}
,
\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{(0)}
,
(r,N,D)
, step size
\eta
, and max iteration
I
.

2:

Compute
w=\|\mathbf{W}_{N}\|_{\text{F}}^{2}

3:

for

j\leftarrow 0
to
I-1

do

4:

\beta_{\text{A}}^{(j+1)}=\beta_{\text{A}}^{(j)}-\eta\cdot\left(\text{tr}(\mathbf{U}^{(j)\top}\mathbf{U}^{(j)}\mathbf{V}^{(j)\top}\mathbf{V}^{(j)})^{-1}\cdot N^{-1}\right)\cdot\nabla_{\beta_{\text{A}}}\mathcal{L}_{T}(\mathbf{\Theta}^{(j)})

5:

\beta_{\text{N}}^{(j+1)}=\beta_{\text{N}}^{(j)}-\eta\cdot\left(\text{tr}(\mathbf{U}^{(j)\top}\mathbf{U}^{(j)}\mathbf{V}^{(j)\top}\mathbf{V}^{(j)})^{-1}\cdot w^{-1}\right)\cdot\nabla_{\beta_{\text{N}}}\mathcal{L}_{T}(\mathbf{\Theta}^{(j)})

6:

\mathbf{U}^{(j+1)}=\mathbf{U}^{(j)}-\eta\cdot(\beta_{\text{A}}^{(j)2}N+\beta_{\text{N}}^{(j)2}w)^{-1}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{U}}\mathcal{L}_{T}(\mathbf{\Theta}^{(j)})\cdot(\mathbf{V}^{(j)\top}\mathbf{V}^{(j)})^{-1}

7:

\mathbf{V}^{(j+1)}=\mathbf{V}^{(j)}-\eta\cdot(\beta_{\text{A}}^{(j)2}N+\beta_{\text{N}}^{(j)2}w)^{-1}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{V}}\mathcal{L}_{T}(\mathbf{\Theta}^{(j)})\cdot(\mathbf{U}^{(j)\top}\mathbf{U}^{(j)})^{-1}

8:

end

for

9:

Return:

\widehat{\mathbf{\Theta}}=(\beta_{\textup{A}}^{(I)},\beta_{\textup{N}}^{(I)},\mathbf{U}^{(I)},\mathbf{V}^{(I)})
.

3.3
Initialization Strategy

Given the non-convex nature of the loss function, ScaledGD requires a warm start to guarantee convergence to the global optimum. We employ an Alternating Least Squares (ALS) approach that exploits the conditional convexity of the problem.

First, initialize node parameters
(\beta_{\text{A}},\beta_{\text{N}})
uniformly on
(0,1)
such that
\beta_{\text{A}}+\beta_{\text{N}}<1
. Construct
\mathbf{B}_{\text{net}}=\beta_{\text{A}}\mathbf{I}_{N}+\beta_{\text{N}}\mathbf{W}_{N}
. Define the predictor matrix
\mathbf{X}_{t}=\mathbf{B}_{\text{net}}\mathbf{Y}_{t-1}
. We solve for the variable matrix via the constrained problem:

\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}_{\text{var}}=\argmin_{\text{rank}(\mathbf{B}_{\text{var}})\leq r}\sum_{t=2}^{T+1}\|\mathbf{Y}_{t}-\mathbf{X}_{t}\mathbf{B}_{\text{var}}^{\top}\|_{\text{F}}^{2}.

(15)

Following
Reinsel and Velu, 1998
, the solution is obtained by a partial eigen-decomposition. Let
\mathbf{S}_{yx}=\sum_{t=2}^{T+1}\mathbf{Y}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{t}
and
\mathbf{S}_{xx}=\sum_{t=2}^{T+1}\mathbf{X}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{t}
. We compute the
r
leading eigenvectors
\mathbf{\Gamma}
of
\mathbf{S}_{yx}\mathbf{S}_{xx}^{-1}\mathbf{S}_{yx}^{\top}
, and extract
\mathbf{U}^{(0)}
and
\mathbf{V}^{(0)}
via the SVD of
\mathbf{\Gamma}\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\top}\mathbf{S}_{yx}\mathbf{S}_{xx}^{-1}
.

Second, given
\mathbf{B}_{\text{var}}
, we apply the OLS estimate to update
\beta_{\text{A}}
and
\beta_{\text{N}}
by

(\widetilde{\beta}_{\text{A}},\widetilde{\beta}_{\text{N}})^{\top}=\left(\sum_{t=2}^{T+1}\mathbf{Z}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{Z}_{t}\right)^{-1}\left(\sum_{t=2}^{T+1}\mathbf{Z}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t}\right),

(16)

where
\mathbf{Z}_{t}=(\mathbf{B}_{\text{var}}\mathbf{Y}_{t-1}^{\top}\otimes\mathbf{I}_{N})[\text{vec}(\mathbf{I}_{N}),\text{vec}(\mathbf{W}_{N})]
and
\mathbf{y}_{t}=\text{vec}(\mathbf{Y}_{t})
. A single iteration of these steps typically yields a basin of attraction sufficient for ScaledGD.

3.4
Rank Selection Method

In applications where the rank
r
is unknown, we employ a singular value ratio criterion. This approach exploits the spectral gap between signal-driven and noise-driven singular values.

We specify an upper bound
\bar{r}<D
and estimate the model with rank constraint
\bar{r}
. Let
\widetilde{\sigma}_{1}\geq\dots\geq\widetilde{\sigma}_{\bar{r}}
denote the singular values of the estimated matrix
\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}_{\text{var}}(\bar{r})
. The rank is estimated as:

\widehat{r}=\argmin_{1\leq j<\bar{r}}\frac{\widetilde{\sigma}_{j+1}+s(D,T)}{\widetilde{\sigma}_{j}+s(D,T)},

(17)

where
s(D,T)=\sqrt{D\log(T)/T}
is a stabilization ridge term. This estimator, consistent with
Wang et al., 2022
, identifies the index
j
where the decay in singular values stabilizes, indicating the transition to the noise floor. Provided that
\bar{r}>r
, the estimator is robust to the choice of
\bar{r}
.

4
Computational and Statistical Convergence Analysis

This section establishes the theoretical properties of the proposed estimator. We first provide a local linear convergence guarantee for the ScaledGD algorithm in Section
4.1
. We then derive non-asymptotic error bounds for the estimation of the transition matrix
\mathbf{A}
and its constituent components in Section
4.2
. Finally, we establish the consistency of the rank selection procedure in Section
4.3
.

4.1
Computational Convergence Analysis

The optimization problem in (
13
) is non-convex due to the bilinear parameter interaction. For
\mathbf{A}=(\mathbf{U}\mathbf{V}^{\top})\otimes(\beta_{\text{A}}\mathbf{I}_{N}+\beta_{\text{N}}\mathbf{W}_{N})
, denote the corresponding loss function with respect to
\mathbf{A}
as
\overline{\mathcal{L}}(\mathbf{A})
such that
\mathcal{L}_{T}(\mathbf{\Theta})=\overline{\mathcal{L}}((\mathbf{U}\mathbf{V}^{\top})\otimes(\beta_{\text{A}}\mathbf{I}_{N}+\beta_{\text{N}}\mathbf{W}_{N}))
.
To analyze the convergence of Algorithm
1
, we impose standard curvature conditions on the loss surface near the truth.

Definition 1

(Restricted Strong Convexity and Smoothness)
.

The loss function
\overline{\mathcal{L}}(\mathbf{A})
satisfies Restricted Strongly Convexity
\mathrm{(RSC)}
with parameter
\alpha>0
and Restricted Strongly Smoothness
\mathrm{(RSS)}
with parameter
\beta>0
if, for any matrix
\mathbf{A}
of the form
\mathbf{A}=(\mathbf{U}\mathbf{V}^{\top})\otimes(\beta_{\textup{A}}\mathbf{I}_{N}+\beta_{\textup{N}}\mathbf{W}_{N})
and the truth
\mathbf{A}^{*}=(\mathbf{U}^{*}\mathbf{V}^{*\top})\otimes(\beta_{\textup{A}}^{*}\mathbf{I}_{N}+\beta_{\textup{N}}^{*}\mathbf{W}_{N})
,

\frac{\alpha}{2}\left\|\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{A}^{*}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\leq\overline{\mathcal{L}}(\mathbf{A})-\overline{\mathcal{L}}\left(\mathbf{A}^{*}\right)-\left\langle\nabla\overline{\mathcal{L}}\left(\mathbf{A}^{*}\right),\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{A}^{*}\right\rangle\leq\frac{\beta}{2}\left\|\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{A}^{*}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}.

These conditions ensure that the loss function is locally quadratic restricted to the proposed parameter space. Such conditions are standard in the analysis of high-dimensional
M
-estimators
(
Negahban et al., 2012
;
Jain and Kar, 2017
)
. For high-dimensional time series analysis, they have been established under various dependency and distributional settings
(
Basu and Michailidis, 2015
;
Wu and Wu, 2016
;
Wang et al., 2024
)
. We verify this condition holds for our model under mild conditions in next subsection.

The statistical error of the estimator is governed by the behavior of the score function at the truth. We define the deviation bound
\xi
as the maximal projection of the noise onto the normalized tangent space of the parameter manifold, as in the standard framework for high-dimensional
M
estimators
(
Negahban et al., 2012
;
Han et al., 2022
)
. The detailed rate
\xi
for the RRNAR model is presented in next subsection.

Definition 2

(Deviation Bound)
.

For a given rank
r
and the true parameter matrices
\mathbf{A}^{*}
, the deviation bound is defined as

\xi:=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{U},\mathbf{V}\in\mathbb{R}^{D\times r},~\beta_{\textup{A}},\beta_{\textup{N}}\in\mathbb{R},\\
\|\mathbf{U}\|_{\textup{F}}=\|{\mathbf{V}}\|_{\textup{F}}=\|\beta_{\textup{A}}\mathbf{I}_{N}+\beta_{\textup{N}}\mathbf{W}_{N}\|_{\mathrm{F}}=1\end{subarray}}\left\langle\nabla\overline{\mathcal{L}}(\mathbf{A}^{*}),\ \mathbf{U}\mathbf{V}^{\top}\otimes(\beta_{\textup{A}}\mathbf{I}_{N}+\beta_{\textup{N}}\mathbf{W}_{N})\right\rangle.

(18)

Let
\phi:=\|\mathbf{B}_{\textup{net}}^{*}\|_{\mathrm{F}}=\|\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}}^{*}\|_{\mathrm{F}}
denote the balanced norm of the true parameters. The following theorem establishes the convergence of the ScaledGD iterates.

Theorem 1

(Local Linear Convergence)
.

Suppose
\overline{\mathcal{L}}
satisfies RSS and RSC conditions with parameters
\alpha
and
\beta
, respectively. There exist universal constants
C>0
and
\eta_{0}\in(0,1/64]
such that if
\xi\leq C\alpha^{2}\beta^{-1}\phi\sigma_{r}(\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}}^{*})
, the initialization error satisfies

\textup{dist}(\mathbf{\Theta}^{(0)},\mathbf{\Theta}^{*})^{2}\leq C\alpha\beta^{-1}\phi^{2}\sigma_{r}^{2}(\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}}^{*})
, and the step size is chosen as
\eta=\eta_{0}\beta^{-1}
, then for all iterations
j\geq 1
, the ScaledGD iterates satisfy

\textup{dist}(\mathbf{\Theta}^{(j)},\mathbf{\Theta}^{*})^{2}\leq(1-C\eta_{0}\alpha\beta^{-1})^{j}\cdot\textup{dist}(\mathbf{\Theta}^{(0)},\mathbf{\Theta}^{*})^{2}+C\eta_{0}\alpha^{-2}\xi^{2},

(19)

and

\left\|\mathbf{A}^{(j)}-\mathbf{A}^{*}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\lesssim(1-C\eta_{0}\alpha\beta^{-1})^{j}\left\|\mathbf{A}^{(0)}-\mathbf{A}^{*}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}+\eta_{0}\alpha^{-2}\xi^{2}.

(20)

In addition,
\mathbf{U}^{(j)}
and
\mathbf{V}^{(j)}
are nonsingular for all
j\geq 1
.

Theorem
1
decomposes the total error into an optimization error, which decays exponentially, and a statistical error floor proportional to
\xi^{2}
. This implies that ScaledGD efficiently reaches the statistical precision of the model. Notably, the convergence rate depends on the ratio
\alpha/\beta
but is independent of the system dimensions
N
and
D
, ensuring scalability.

4.2
Statistical Convergence Analysis

We now quantify the statistical error
\xi
and derive explicit bounds for the model parameters. We posit the following assumptions on the data generating process.

Assumption 1

(Stationarity)
.

The spectral radius of the transition matrix satisfies
\rho(\mathbf{A}^{*})<1
, ensuring a unique strictly stationary solution.

Assumption 2

(Gaussian Noise)
.

The vectorized error process
\mathrm{vec}({\mathbf{E}_{t}})
is a sequence of independent and identically distributed Gaussian vectors with mean zero and positive definite covariance
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{e}}
, and
\mathbf{E}_{t}
is independent of the historical observations
\{\mathbf{Y}_{s}\}_{s<t}
.

Assumption
1
ensures the stationarity of the process.
Assumption
2
facilitates the derivation of sharp concentration inequalities
(
Basu and Michailidis, 2015
;
Wang et al., 2022
)
but can be relaxed to sub-Gaussian distributions at the cost of distinct constant factors.

To characterize the temporal dependence, define the operator
\mathcal{A}(z)=\mathbf{I}_{ND}-\mathbf{A}^{z}
for
z\in\mathbb{C}
. Let
\mu_{\min}(\mathcal{A})
and
\mu_{\max}(\mathcal{A})
denote the minimum and maximum eigenvalues of the spectral density matrix
\mathcal{A}^{\dagger}(z)\mathcal{A}(z)
on the unit circle
|z|=1
, where
\dagger
denotes the conjugate transpose. We define the following signal-to-noise ratios and stability constants:

\alpha_{\textup{RSC}}=\frac{\sigma_{\max}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{e}})}{2\mu_{\max}(\mathcal{A})},~\beta_{\textup{RSS}}=\frac{3\sigma_{\max}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{e}})}{2\mu_{\max}(\mathcal{A})},~M_{1}:=\frac{\sigma_{\max}\left(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{e}}\right)\mu_{\max}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)}{\sigma_{\min}\left(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{e}}\right)\mu_{\min}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)},~\text{and}~M_{2}:=\frac{\sigma_{\max}\left(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{e}}\right)}{\mu_{\min}^{1/2}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)},

(21)

where
\sigma_{\max}(\cdot)
and
\sigma_{\min}(\cdot)
denote the largest and smallest eigenvalue, respectively.

Theorem 2

(Statistical Rate for
\mathbf{A}
)
.

Under Assumptions
1
and
2
, if the sample size satisfies
T\gtrsim M_{1}^{2}Dr
, then with probability at least
1-C\exp(-CDr)
, the RSC and RSS conditions in Definition
1
hold with
\alpha=\alpha_{\textup{RSC}}
and
\beta=\beta_{\textup{RSS}}
, respectively, and the deviation bound in Definition
2
is bounded by

\xi\lesssim M_{2}\sqrt{Dr/T}.

(22)

Furthermore, under the conditions of Theorem
1
with
\alpha=\alpha_{\textup{RSC}}
and
\beta=\beta_{\textup{RSS}}
, with probability at least
1-C\exp(-CDr)
, the ScaledGD output after sufficient iterations satisfies

\left\|\widehat{\mathbf{B}}_{\textup{var}}\otimes\widehat{\mathbf{B}}_{\textup{net}}-\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}}^{*}\otimes\mathbf{B}_{\textup{net}}^{*}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\lesssim\alpha_{\textup{RSC}}^{-2}M_{2}^{2}\cdot(Dr/T).

(23)

Theorem
2
highlights the efficiency of the reduced-rank network specification. Although the ambient dimension of
\mathbf{A}
is
(ND)^{2}
, the convergence rate is governed by the effective degrees of freedom
(Dr)
. Crucially, the rate is independent of the number of nodes
N
, suggesting the model is well-suited for large-scale networks, provided
T
grows with the variable dimension
D
.

We next provide component-wise error bounds. As discussed in Section
2.4
,
\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}}
and
\mathbf{B}_{\textup{net}}
are identified only up to scale, and
\mathbf{U},\mathbf{V}
only up to rotation. We impose the balancing constraint
\|\widehat{\mathbf{B}}_{\textup{var}}\|_{\mathrm{F}}=\|\widehat{\mathbf{B}}_{\textup{net}}\|_{\mathrm{F}}
to fix the scale and measure the accuracy of
\mathbf{U}
and
\mathbf{V}
via the Frobenius distance between their projection matrices,
\mathcal{P}_{\widehat{\mathbf{U}}}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\widehat{\mathbf{V}}}
, where
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{M}}=\mathbf{M}(\mathbf{M}^{\top}\mathbf{M})^{-1}\mathbf{M}^{\top}
is the orthogonal projection matrix for the column spaces of any matrix
\mathbf{M}
.

Corollary 1

(Component-wise Rates)
.

Under the conditions of Theorem
2
, with probability at least
1-C\exp(-CDr)
, the component estimators satisfy

\displaystyle(\widehat{\beta}_{\textup{A}}-\beta_{\textup{A}}^{*})^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\phi^{-2}M_{2}^{2}\alpha_{\mathrm{RSC}}^{-2}\cdot DrN^{-1}T^{-1},

(24)

\displaystyle(\widehat{\beta}_{\textup{N}}-\beta_{\textup{N}}^{*})^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\phi^{-2}M_{2}^{2}\alpha_{\mathrm{RSC}}^{-2}\cdot Dr\|\mathbf{W}_{N}\|_{\textup{F}}^{-2}T^{-1},

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{P}_{\widehat{\mathbf{U}}}-\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{U}^{*}}\|_{\textup{F}}^{2}\asymp\|\mathcal{P}_{\widehat{\mathbf{V}}}-\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{V}^{*}}\|_{\textup{F}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\phi^{-2}\sigma_{r}^{-2}(\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}}^{*})M_{2}^{2}\alpha_{\mathrm{RSC}}^{-2}\cdot DrT^{-1}.

Corollary
1
reveals a mechanism of variance reduction via network aggregation. For a fixed
D
, the estimation error for the autoregressive parameter
\beta_{\textup{A}}
decays at rate
(NT)^{-1}
, and for the network parameter
\beta_{\textup{N}}
at rate
(\|\mathbf{W}_{N}\|_{\text{F}}^{2}T)^{-1}
. Since
\|\mathbf{W}_{N}\|_{\text{F}}^{2}
typically grows with
N
(e.g.,
\|\mathbf{W}_{N}\|_{\text{F}}\propto\sqrt{N}
in sparse random graphs), the precision of the node-level dynamics improves as the network size increases. This confirms that the pooling of information across nodes allows for consistent estimation of global network effects even when
T
is moderate.

4.3
Rank Selection Consistency

We establish the consistency of the rank selection criterion in (
17
). We analyze the asymptotic regime where
T,D\to\infty
while the true rank
r
remains fixed.

Theorem 3

(Rank Selection Consistency)
.

Under the Assumption
1
and
2
, if
T\gtrsim M_{1}^{2}D\bar{r}
,
\bar{r}>r
,
\phi^{-1}\alpha_{\mathrm{RSC}}^{-1}M_{2}\sqrt{D\bar{r}/T}=o(s(D,T))
, and
s(D,T)=o(\sigma^{-1}_{r}\min_{1\leq j\leq r-1}\sigma_{j+1}/\sigma_{j})
, then we have
\mathbb{P}(\widehat{r}=r)\to 1
, as
T,D\to\infty
.

Assuming the constants
\alpha_{\mathrm{RSC}}^{-1}
,
\phi
,
\sigma_{1}
,
\sigma_{r}^{-1}
, and
M_{2}
are bounded, the two conditions in Theorem
3
simplify to: (1)
s(D,T)^{-1}\sqrt{D\overline{r}/T}\to 0
, and (2)
s(D,T)\to 0
. The first condition requires the ridge parameter
s(D,T)
to vanish slower than the estimation error rate
O(\sqrt{D\overline{r}/T})
. Our choice
s(D,T)=\sqrt{D\log(T)/T}
satisfies this, as
\sqrt{\overline{r}/\log T}\to 0
. The second condition,
s(D,T)\to 0
, ensures the ridge parameter itself converges to zero. Our choice of
s(D,T)
satisfies this second condition if and only if we assume an asymptotic regime where
D\log(T)=o(T)
.
Moreover, the required sample size in Theorem
3
is the same as that for the estimation consistency in Theorem
2
.

5
Simulation Studies

We conduct two Monte Carlo simulation experiments to evaluate the finite-sample performance of the RRNAR estimator. We focus on validating the consistency of the rank selection procedure and verifying the non-asymptotic convergence rates derived in Section
4.2
.

In both experiments, we generate data from the RRNAR model
\mathbf{Y}_{t}=\mathbf{B}_{\textup{net}}\mathbf{Y}_{t-1}\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}}^{\top}+\mathbf{E}_{t}
with
\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{E}_{t})\sim N(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{ND})
. A burn-in period of 100 observations is discarded to ensure stationarity. The network topology is a
k
-regular directed cyclic graph. The true parameters are generated as follows. For the network operator,
\mathbf{B}_{\text{net}}=\beta_{\text{A}}\mathbf{I}_{N}+\beta_{\text{N}}\mathbf{W}_{N}
, where
\beta_{\text{A}},\beta_{\text{N}}\sim U(0,1)
, subject to stationarity. For the variable operator,
\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}}=\mathbf{Q}_{1}\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{Q}_{2}^{\top}
, where
\mathbf{Q}_{1},\mathbf{Q}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{D\times r}
are random orthonormal matrices and
\mathbf{\Lambda}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times r}
is a diagonal matrix with entries sampled from
U[0.5,1.5]
. Parameters are re-sampled if the stationarity condition
\rho(\mathbf{B}_{\textup{net}})\rho(\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}})\geq 1
is violated.

5.1
Experiment I: Consistency of Rank Selection

We examine the singular value ratio estimator in (
17
) across a grid of dimensions:
N\in\{20,50,100\}
,
D\in\{10,20,50\}
, and
r\in\{2,3\}
. We set the search upper bound
\bar{r}=\min(D/2,10)
and the ridge term
s(D,T)=0.5\sqrt{D\log(T)/T}
. Table
1
reports the selection accuracy over 500 replications. The results confirm the consistency result in Theorem
3
. Accuracy approaches 100% as
T
increases, and importantly, the method remains robust in high-dimensional settings (
D=50,N=100
) provided
T\geq 800
.

5.2
Experiment II: Verification of Theoretical Rates

We validate the convergence rates derived in Corollary
1
, specifically the dependencies on
N
,
D
and
T
. First, to verify that network sparsity drives variance reduction for scalar parameters, we compare two topologies: (1) sparse (
k=3
,
\|\mathbf{W}_{N}\|_{\textup{F}}^{2}\propto N
) and (2) dense (
k=\lfloor N/2\rfloor
,
\|\mathbf{W}_{N}\|_{\textup{F}}^{2}\propto 1
). We vary
N
from 10 to 200 with
T=500,D=6,r=2
. We control for dynamic stability across
N
by fixing the true scalar coefficients
\beta_{\text{A}}^{*}
and
\beta_{\text{N}}^{*}
. Since
\mathbf{W}_{N}
is row-normalized, the spectral radius
\rho(\mathbf{B}_{\text{net}}^{*})=\beta_{\text{A}}^{*}+\beta_{\text{N}}^{*}
remains invariant to
N
, ensuring that performance differences are attributable solely to dimensionality. We report
rescaled estimation errors
(scaled by nuisance parameters
\phi^{2}
and
\sigma_{r}^{2}
) to isolate the convergence rates.

Figure
1
presents the results on a log-log scale. In panels (a) and (b), the errors for the global matrix
\mathbf{A}
and the subspaces
\mathbf{U},\mathbf{V}
show no trend with respect to
N
. This confirms that the estimation of the high-dimensional variable dynamics is independent of the network size. In panels (c) and (d), the error for
\beta_{\text{A}}
decays linearly (
\mathcal{O}(N^{-1})
) for both topologies. Crucially, the error for
\beta_{\text{N}}
exhibits the blessing of dimensionality (slope
\approx-1
) only in the sparse setting. In the dense setting, the error plateaus. These findings perfectly confirm the rate
\mathcal{O}(\|\mathbf{W}_{N}\|_{\textup{F}}^{-2}T^{-1})
in Corollary
1
.

Figure 1
:
Estimation errors ((a) and (b)) or log errors ((c) and (d)) versus
N
. Blue and red colors denote sparse (
k=3
) and dense (
k=\lfloor N/2\rfloor
) topologies, respectively.

Next, we evaluate the dependence on the feature dimension
D\in\{5,\dots,200\}
with fixed
N=15,T=500,r=3
. To ensure comparability, we fix the singular value spectrum
\mathbf{\Lambda}^{*}
across dimensions, keeping the total signal strength
\|\mathbf{B}_{\text{var}}^{*}\|_{\text{F}}
constant. Figure
2
confirms that estimation complexity scales linearly with
D
. In panels (a) and (b), the overall error (
\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}}\otimes\mathbf{B}_{\textup{net}}
) and projection errors (
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{U}}
,
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{V}}
) exhibit a precise linear trend (
R^{2}\approx 0.99
), validating the
\mathcal{O}(Dr)
rate. In panels (c) and (d), the errors for
\beta_{\text{A}}
and
\beta_{\text{N}}
also scale linearly with
D
. The slight oscillation in these estimates arises because they are identified via the norm-balancing constraint; thus, noise from the high-dimensional estimator
\widehat{\mathbf{B}}_{\text{var}}
propagates to the scalars.

Figure 2
:
Impact of Feature Dimension
D
on Estimation Accuracy.

Finally, we assess consistency with respect to sample size
T\in\{100,\dots,10000\}
for a fixed model (
N=50,D=30,r=3
). Figure
3
shows the estimation error
\|\widehat{\mathbf{A}}-\mathbf{A}^{*}\|_{\text{F}}^{2}
on a log-log scale. The error decays linearly with
T^{-1}
, confirming that the estimator is statistically consistent and achieves the parametric rate
{\mathcal{O}}(1/T)
in the large-sample regime.

Figure 3
:
Impact of Sample Size
T
.

6
Real Data Example

In this section, we evaluate the proposed RRNAR model on two real-world datasets characterized by distinct dimensionalities and network topologies. The primary objective is to demonstrate that explicitly modeling the coupling between network spillovers and low-rank cross-variable interactions yields superior forecasting accuracy and structural interpretability compared to existing methods.

We benchmark the performance of the RRNAR model against four strong benchmarks in high-dimensional time series analysis.

•

NAR

(
Zhu et al., 2017
)
: A univariate network autoregressive model applied component-wise. This baseline tests the necessity of modeling cross-variable dependencies.

•

MAR

(
Chen et al., 2021
)
: A matrix autoregressive model with full-rank transition matrices. This tests the benefit of rank reduction and network constraints.

•

RRMAR

(
Xiao et al., 2022
)
: A reduced-rank MAR model that ignores the physical graph, learning spatial structure entirely from data.

•

RRVAR

(
Reinsel and Velu, 1998
)
: A vectorized multivariate model that ignores the matrix structure entirely, serving as a baseline for the curse of dimensionality.

•

RRNAR (Proposed)
: The rank
r
is selected via the consistent ratio criterion in (
17
).

Forecasting is conducted using a rolling window scheme. We report the Mean Squared Error (MSE) for each variable, averaged over all nodes and time points in the test set, as well as the global MSE taking average of three variables after normalization.

6.1
Application I: Traffic Flow Network

We first analyze the PeMS08 dataset, which comprises traffic data from
N=170
sensors in the San Bernardino area (data can be downloaded at
https://github.com/Davidham3/STSGCN
). Each sensor records
D=3
variables: flow, occupancy, and speed. The data is aggregated into 1-hour intervals, yielding
T=1488
observations. This dataset represents a scenario with a rigid physical topology and highly correlated variables. The adjacency matrix
\mathbf{W}_{N}
is constructed using a Gaussian kernel on road network distances with a sparsity threshold; see details in Appendix
G
of Supplementary Materials.

Then we examine the topological structure of the sensor network encoded in the adjacency matrix
\mathbf{W}_{N}
. The constructed matrix is highly sparse with a density of only 0.96%, reflecting the localized nature of traffic propagation. From the preceding theoretical analysis, we can know that this high sparsity is precisely more beneficial for constructing the RRNAR model, enabling efficient forecasting by filtering out noise from irrelevant distant sensors and focusing exclusively on informative local neighborhoods. As illustrated in the degree distribution histograms (Figure
4
), the weighted degrees exhibit a heavily right-skewed pattern where the majority of nodes possess limited connectivity while a few hub nodes act as central aggregators. This significant structural heterogeneity underscores the necessity of the proposed network operator
\mathbf{B}_{\text{net}}
, as a uniform autoregressive assumption (e.g., MAR, VAR) would fail to capture these localized and uneven spatial dependencies.

Figure 4
:
Histogram of degrees in the PeMS08 sensor network. The left panel shows the out-degrees for
N=170
nodes; The right panel shows a similar histogram but for in-degrees.

To further explore the benefits of incorporating into the network operator
\mathbf{B}_{\text{net}}
, we investigate whether expanding the network scale enhances the estimation precision. We conducted a subnetwork sampling experiment by training the model on connected subgraphs with sizes
N
ranging from 20 to 160. Figure
5(a)
plots the standard deviation of the estimated parameters across 50 independent trials. The variance of the network spillover effect
\widehat{\beta}_{\text{N}}
exhibits a rapid decay as the subnetwork size increases, which empirically validating our theoretical blessing of dimensionality. This observation demonstrates that the RRNAR model effectively capitalizes on the richer structural information provided by larger graph topologies to stabilize the inference of global network effects, ultimately achieving superior asymptotic robustness compared to the baseline autoregressive estimation.

(a)

Variance of
\widehat{\beta}
vs
N

(b)

Visualization of
\widehat{\mathbf{B}}_{\text{var}}
.

Figure 5
:
The PeMS08 dataset analysis. The left panel: variance of
\widehat{\beta}
vs
N
; The right panel: heatmap visualization of the estimated variable interaction matrix
\widehat{\mathbf{B}}_{\text{var}}
.

We also investigate the cross-variable dependency mechanisms captured by the estimated model parameters to validate their interpretability. As shown in the Figure
5(b)
, the dominant positive diagonal elements are observed in the variable interaction matrix
\widehat{\mathbf{B}}_{\text{var}}
. Crucially, the model successfully uncovers the physical laws governing traffic flow as evidenced by the significant negative coefficient from lagged occupancy to current speed. This empirical finding aligns with the fundamental diagram of traffic flow where increased road occupancy leads to congestion and a subsequent reduction in vehicle speed. This demonstrates that the RRNAR model captures meaningful cross-variable interactions beyond simple univariate autoregression (e.g., NAR).

Finally, we investigate the estimation performance of different models. We use the first 1190 steps for training and the final 298 steps for testing. Table
2
summarizes the forecasting accuracy. The RRNAR model achieves the lowest global MSE, outperforming the univariate NAR baseline by approximately 10.0%. The inferior performance of the network-ignorant models (RRVAR, MAR) confirms that traffic states are spatially dependent and that the physical graph provides essential regularization.

Figure

visualizes the forecast for a representative node during a volatile period. The univariate NAR model (blue dashed) exhibits a characteristic phase lag, consistently underestimating the onset of sudden congestion. In contrast, RRNAR (red solid) aligns closely with the ground truth. This phase correction is attributable to the multivariate network structure: by leveraging occupancy spikes in upstream sensors as precursors via the low-rank factor space, RRNAR anticipates regime shifts more rapidly than the scalar-based baseline.

6.2
Application II: Server Cluster Monitoring

The Server Machine Dataset (SMD) represents a high-dimensional system with
N=28
machines, each monitored by
D=38
metrics (e.g., CPU load, memory usage) (data can be downloaded at
https://github.com/NetManAIOps/OmniAnomaly
). The data is downsampled to 10-minute intervals (
T=2368
). Unlike the traffic case, the network
\mathbf{W}_{N}
is defined by functional dependencies: an edge exists if the pairwise correlation of CPU usage in the training set exceeds 0.5. Details are given in Appendix
G
of Supplementary Materials.

The constructed symmetric adjacency matrix
\mathbf{W}_{N}
has a density of 6.35%, and its degree distribution shown in the Figure
7(a)
again exhibits a long-tail pattern, confirming the structural heterogeneity inherent in server clusters. We also conducted a subnetwork sampling experiment on the SMD to validate the estimator under a high-dimensional regime (
D=38
) relative to the network size. Figure
7(b)
reports the standard deviation of the estimated parameters as the subnetwork size
N
increases. Both metrics exhibit a strictly monotonic decay, empirically confirming the blessing of dimensionality, even in this feature-rich setting. Notably, the estimation uncertainty for the network parameter
\widehat{\beta}_{N}
(blue) remains consistently higher than that of the autoregressive parameter
\widehat{\beta}_{A}
(red). This contrasts with the traffic dataset results and arises from the smaller size of the server cluster (
N=28
). Unlike the large traffic grid (
N=170
), the server network offers fewer nodes to average out noise, making the global network effect more difficult to identify than local autoregressive dynamics. However, the sharp drop in variance as
N
increases confirms that the RRNAR model successfully overcomes this small-sample challenge to achieve robust inference.

(a)

Histogram of degrees for
N=28
nodes

(b)

Variance of
\widehat{\beta}
vs
N
.

Figure 7
:
The Server Machine Dataset analysis. The left panel: histogram of degrees for
N=28
nodes and the
\mathbf{W}_{N}
is symmetrical; The right panel: variance of
\widehat{\beta}
vs
N
.

To investigate the internal mechanisms driving these dynamics, we visualize the latent factor structure of the estimated variable interaction matrix
\widehat{\mathbf{B}}_{\text{var}}=\widehat{\mathbf{U}}\widehat{\mathbf{V}}^{\top}
(Figure
8
). The first factor identifies an “I/O-Bound Bottleneck.” Its formation pattern at
t-1
(input
\widehat{\mathbf{V}}
) shows high Disk Utilization with low Memory Paging, indicating active data retrieval. This state strongly suppresses the Network Module at time
t
(output
\widehat{\mathbf{U}}
), confirming that saturated disk bandwidth physically throttles network throughput
(
Ma et al., 2014
)
. The second factor captures a “Kernel-to-User Scheduling Cycle.” The input pattern highlights a trade-off where System Load is high while User Load is suppressed (a kernel scheduling phase), which drives a subsequent surge in User Load at time
t

(
Markovic et al., 2015
)
. By accurately modeling these time-lagged physical dependencies, RRNAR disentangles complex operational mechanisms from high-dimensional monitoring data.

Figure 8
:
Visualization of the latent factor structure of the variable interaction matrix
\widehat{\mathbf{B}}_{\text{var}}
. The left panel displays the formation patterns (input loadings
\widehat{\mathbf{V}}
at
t-1
), representing the driver state; the right panel displays the impact patterns (output loadings
\widehat{\mathbf{U}}
at
t
), representing the system response. Factor 1 captures an “I/O-Bound Bottleneck”, Factor 2 identifies a “Kernel-to-User Scheduling Cycle”.

Table
3
summarizes the global performance between different models with a chronological 8:2 split of training and testing data. A key observation is the highly right-skewed distribution of prediction errors (median
\ll
mean), indicating that the primary challenge lies in predicting sparse, high-magnitude anomalies rather than stable baselines. In this context, while MAR achieves a low median, its high mean suggests it overfits to stable periods but fails during volatile fluctuations.

:

The RRNAR model achieves the lowest global MSE (
4.47\times 10^{-4}
), outperforming the strongest baseline (NAR) by 5.9%. This improvement suggests that anomalies in the cluster are driven by latent systemic factors, such as a computation-intensive task that simultaneously impacts CPU and power consumption across functionally linked machines. By explicitly modeling these factors via low-rank constraints, RRNAR filters out idiosyncratic noise, resulting in superior robustness.

7
Conclusion and Discussion

This article introduces the Reduced-Rank Network Autoregressive (RRNAR) model for high-dimensional multivariate network time series. By coupling a rigid network operator with a flexible low-rank variable subspace, the model successfully captures cross-channel spillovers while mitigating the curse of dimensionality. We developed a Scaled Gradient Descent algorithm to handle the geometric ill-conditioning of the parameter space and established theoretical bounds demonstrating a blessing of dimensionality for the network parameters. We mainly focus on the lag-1 RRNAR model and relegate the general lag-
L
extensions to Appendix
E
of Supplementary Materials.

Several promising directions for future research remain. First, our current model assumes homogeneous effects across all nodes. In practice, this assumption could be relaxed by grouping nodes based on domain-specific structures, such as geographical regions in traffic sensor networks or functional roles in server clusters. Suppose that the
N
nodes are classified into
K
distinct groups. A
Group-RRNAR
extension is defined as:

\mathbf{Y}_{t}=(\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\text{A}}+\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\text{N}}\mathbf{W}_{N})\mathbf{Y}_{t-1}\mathbf{B}_{\text{var}}^{\top}+\mathbf{E}_{t},

(25)

where
\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\text{A}}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\beta_{\text{A}k}\mathbf{S}_{k}
and
\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\text{N}}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\beta_{\text{N}k}\mathbf{S}_{k}
are diagonal coefficient matrices. Here,
\mathbf{S}_{k}\in\{0,1\}^{N\times N}
denotes the diagonal selection matrix for the
k
-th group (with
\sum_{k=1}^{K}\mathbf{S}_{k}=\mathbf{I}_{N}
), where the
(i,i)
-th entry is 1 if node
i
belongs to group
k
, and 0 otherwise. Second, the current framework relies on a static network topology
\mathbf{W}_{N}
. Extending the model to accommodate time-varying or adaptive networks would be valuable for applications like social media analysis, where the graph structure evolves endogenously with the node states. Finally, while we focus on point forecasting, incorporating heavy-tailed error distributions or constructing conformal prediction intervals would enhance its utility for risk management in financial and infrastructure systems.

Supplementary Materials to “Reduced-Rank Autoregressive Model for High-Dimensional Multivariate Network Time Series”

Appendix A
Technical Details and Gradients Analysis

In this appendix, we present the details, including the mathematical derivation of partial gradients of Algorithm
1
and the explanation of the gradients scaling ambiguities.

A.1
Algorithm Details

In this section, we provide detailed expressions of the gradients in Algorithm
1
omitted in the main article. The target of Algorithm
1
is to minimize
\mathcal{L}_{T}
in (
13
) with the given
r
:

\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}:=\argmin\mathcal{L}_{T}(\mathbf{\Theta})=\argmin\left\{\frac{1}{2T}\sum_{t=2}^{T+1}\|\mathbf{Y}_{t}-(\beta_{\textup{A}}\mathbf{I}_{N}+\beta_{\textup{N}}\mathbf{W}_{N})\mathbf{Y}_{t-1}\mathbf{V}\mathbf{U}^{\top}\|_{\text{F}}^{2}\right\}.

(A.1)

To derive the expressions of the gradients, we give some useful notations. For any matrices
\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
and
\mathbf{N}\in\mathbb{R}^{D\times D}
, since all entries of
\mathbf{M}\otimes\mathbf{N}
and
\text{vec}(\mathbf{M})\text{vec}(\mathbf{N})^{\top}
are the same up to a permutation, we define the entry-wise re-arrangement operation
\mathcal{P}
such that

\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{M}\otimes\mathbf{N})=\text{vec}(\mathbf{M})\text{vec}(\mathbf{N})^{\top},

(A.2)

where the dimensions of the matrix are omitted for simplicity. The reverse operation
\mathcal{P}^{-1}
is defined such that

\mathcal{P}^{-1}(\text{vec}(\mathbf{M})\text{vec}(\mathbf{N})^{\top})=\mathbf{M}\otimes\mathbf{N}.

(A.3)

Also, we use the notation vec(
\cdot
) and mat(
\cdot
) to denote the vectorization operator and its inverse.

The proposed RRNAR(1) model is written as

\mathbf{Y}_{t}=\mathbf{B}_{\textup{net}}\mathbf{Y}_{t-1}\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}}^{\top}+\mathbf{E}_{t},\quad t=2,\dots,T+1,

(A.4)

where
\mathbf{B}_{\textup{net}}=\beta_{\textup{A}}\mathbf{I}_{N}+\beta_{\textup{N}}\mathbf{W}_{N}
and
\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}}=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{V}^{\top}

can be vectorized and rewritten as

\mathbf{y}_{t}=(\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}}\otimes\mathbf{B}_{\textup{net}})\mathbf{y}_{t-1}+\mathbf{e}_{t},\quad t=2,\dots,T+1,

(A.5)

where
\mathbf{y}_{t}:=\text{vec}(\mathbf{Y}_{t})
and
\mathbf{e}_{t}:=\text{vec}(\mathbf{E}_{t})
. We denote
\mathbf{A}:=\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}}\otimes\mathbf{B}_{\textup{net}}
to be the uniquely defined parameter matrix, and we also let
\mathbf{Y}:=(\mathbf{y}_{T+1},\mathbf{y}_{T-1},...,\mathbf{y}_{2})
and
\mathbf{X}:=(\mathbf{y}_{T},\mathbf{y}_{T-2},...,\mathbf{y}_{1})
. Then, the loss function
\mathcal{L}_{T}
can be viewed as a function of
\mathbf{A}
:

\overline{\mathcal{L}}(\mathbf{A}):=\mathcal{L}_{T}(\boldsymbol{\Theta})=\frac{1}{2T}\left\|\mathbf{Y}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{X}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}=\frac{1}{2T}\left\|\mathbf{Y}-(\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}}\otimes\mathbf{B}_{\textup{net}})\mathbf{X}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}.

(A.6)

Now we derive the gradients. First, the gradient of
\overline{\mathcal{L}}
with respect to
\mathbf{A}
is

\nabla\overline{\mathcal{L}}(\mathbf{A}):=\frac{\partial\overline{\mathcal{L}}}{\partial\mathbf{A}}=-\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=2}^{T+1}(\mathbf{y}_{t}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{y}_{t-1})\mathbf{y}_{t-1}^{\top}=-\frac{1}{T}(\mathbf{Y}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{X})\mathbf{X}^{\top}.

(A.7)

Let
\mathbf{b}_{\textup{net}}=\text{vec}(\mathbf{B}_{\textup{net}})
,
\mathbf{b}_{\textup{var}}=\text{vec}(\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}})
, and
\mathbf{B}:=\text{vec}(\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}})\otimes\text{vec}(\mathbf{B}_{\textup{net}})^{\top}=\mathbf{b}_{\textup{var}}\mathbf{b}_{\textup{net}}^{\top}
. Obviously,
\mathbf{A}=\mathcal{P}^{-1}(\mathbf{B})
and
\mathbf{B}=\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{A})
. Define
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}(\mathbf{B})=\overline{\mathcal{L}}(\mathbf{A})
. Based on the entry-wise matrix permutation operations
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{P}^{-1}
, we have

\begin{split}\nabla\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}(\mathbf{B})&=\mathcal{P}(\nabla\overline{\mathcal{L}}(\mathbf{A}))=\mathcal{P}((\mathbf{A}\mathbf{X}-\mathbf{Y})\mathbf{X}^{\top}/T),\\
\nabla_{\mathbf{b}_{\textup{net}}}\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}&=\mathcal{P}((\mathbf{A}\mathbf{X}-\mathbf{Y})\mathbf{X}^{\top}/T)^{\top}\mathbf{b}_{\textup{var}},\\
\nabla_{\mathbf{b}_{\textup{var}}}\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}&=\mathcal{P}((\mathbf{A}\mathbf{X}-\mathbf{Y})\mathbf{X}^{\top}/T)\mathbf{b}_{\textup{net}},\\
\nabla_{\mathbf{B}_{\textup{net}}}\overline{\mathcal{L}}&=\text{mat}(\mathcal{P}((\mathbf{A}\mathbf{X}-\mathbf{Y})\mathbf{X}^{\top}/T)^{\top}\text{vec}(\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}}))\\
&=\text{mat}(\mathcal{P}(\nabla\overline{\mathcal{L}}(\mathbf{A}))^{\top}\text{vec}(\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}})),\\
\text{and }\nabla_{\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}}}\overline{\mathcal{L}}&=\text{mat}(\mathcal{P}((\mathbf{A}\mathbf{X}-\mathbf{Y})\mathbf{X}^{\top}/T)\text{vec}(\mathbf{B}_{\textup{net}}))\\
&=\text{mat}(\mathcal{P}(\nabla\overline{\mathcal{L}}(\mathbf{A}))\text{vec}(\mathbf{B}_{\textup{net}})).\end{split}

(A.8)

The gradients of
\mathcal{L}_{T}
with respect to
\beta_{\textup{A}},\beta_{\textup{N}},\mathbf{U},\mathbf{V}
are:

\begin{split}\nabla_{\beta_{\textup{A}}}\mathcal{L}_{T}(\mathbf{\Theta})&=\left\langle\mathbf{I}_{N},\nabla_{\mathbf{B}_{\textup{net}}}\overline{\mathcal{L}}(\mathbf{A})\right\rangle,~~\nabla_{\beta_{\textup{N}}}\mathcal{L}_{T}(\mathbf{\Theta})=\left\langle\mathbf{W}_{N},\nabla_{\mathbf{B}_{\textup{net}}}\overline{\mathcal{L}}(\mathbf{A})\right\rangle,\\
\nabla_{\mathbf{U}}\mathcal{L}_{T}(\mathbf{\Theta})&=\nabla_{\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}}}\overline{\mathcal{L}}(\mathbf{A})\mathbf{V},~~\text{and}~~\nabla_{\mathbf{V}}\mathcal{L}_{T}(\mathbf{\Theta})=\nabla_{\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}}}\overline{\mathcal{L}}(\mathbf{A})^{\top}\mathbf{U}.\end{split}

(A.9)

A.2
Gradients Scaling Ambiguities

Equations (
A.8
) and (
A.9
) reveal the structural coupling between the different parameter updates.
In particular, the norms of the block gradients satisfy the cross-dependence

\|\nabla_{\mathbf{B}_{\text{net}}}\overline{\mathcal{L}}\|_{\text{F}}\propto\|\mathbf{B}_{\text{var}}\|_{\text{F}},\quad\|\nabla_{\mathbf{B}_{\text{var}}}\overline{\mathcal{L}}\|_{\text{F}}\propto\|\mathbf{B}_{\text{net}}\|_{\text{F}},

and consequently,

|\nabla_{\beta_{\textup{A}}}\mathcal{L}_{T}(\mathbf{\Theta})|\propto\|\mathbf{B}_{\text{var}}\|_{\text{F}}\cdot\|\mathbf{I}_{N}\|_{\text{F}},\quad|\nabla_{\beta_{\textup{N}}}\mathcal{L}_{T}(\mathbf{\Theta})|\propto\|\mathbf{B}_{\text{var}}\|_{\text{F}}\cdot\|\mathbf{W}_{N}\|_{\text{F}},

\|\nabla_{\mathbf{U}}\mathcal{L}_{T}(\mathbf{\Theta})\|_{\text{F}}\propto\|\mathbf{B}_{\text{net}}\|_{\text{F}}\cdot\|\mathbf{V}\|_{\text{F}},\quad\|\nabla_{\mathbf{V}}\mathcal{L}_{T}(\mathbf{\Theta})\|_{\text{F}}\propto\|\mathbf{B}_{\text{net}}\|_{\text{F}}\cdot\|\mathbf{U}\|_{\text{F}}.

Two sources of non-identifiability underlie difficult numerical behavior. First, the bilinear scaling indeterminacy
(\mathbf{B}_{\text{net}},\mathbf{B}_{\text{var}})\to(c\mathbf{B}_{\text{net}},c^{-1}\mathbf{B}_{\text{var}})
leaves
\mathbf{A}
invariant; second, the factorization indeterminacy creates a fundamental gradient imbalance. Hence, by choosing
c\gg 1
, one can inflate
\|\mathbf{B}_{\text{net}}\|_{\text{F}}
while shrinking
\|\mathbf{B}_{\text{var}}\|_{\text{F}}
without changing the loss. As a result, the gradients for node-side parameters
(\beta_{\textup{A}},\beta_{\textup{N}})
can vanish while gradients for variable-side parameters
(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{V})
explode, or vice versa. The rotational indeterminacy further couples
\mathbf{U}
and
\mathbf{V}
and can lead to large, poorly directed updates within the factor subspace. In addition, A significant size difference between
\|\mathbf{I}_{N}\|_{\text{F}}
and
\|\mathbf{W}_{N}\|_{\text{F}}
can also cause gradient updates for
\beta_{\textup{A}}
and
\beta_{\textup{N}}
to become unbalanced.

Consequently, this combination produces exploding gradients in some parameter directions and vanishing gradients in others, rendering a single global learning rate ineffective and causing slow, oscillatory, or unstable convergence of naive gradient descent or alternating gradient schemes.

Appendix B
Computational Convergence Analysis

In this appendix, we present the proof of Theorem
1
, i.e., the computational convergence of scaled gradient decent algorithm proposed in the main article. To make it easier to follow, it is divided into six steps. Auxiliary lemmas and their proofs are listed in Appendix
B.2
.

B.1
Proof of Theorem
1

Step 1.
(Roadmap of the proof)

The proof is organized as follows. In
Step 1
, we give a roadmap of the proof. In
Step 2
, we summarize all the definitions, notations, and conditions used in the proof. We use
\mathrm{dist}^{2}_{(j)}
to represent
\mathrm{dist}(\mathbf{\Theta}^{(j)},\mathbf{\Theta}^{*})^{2}
defined in main article to quantify the estimation error after
j
-th iteration, and use
\xi
defined in the main text to quantify the statistical error. For brevity, we use
\mathbf{B}_{1}
and
\mathbf{B}_{2}
to represent
\mathbf{B}_{\textup{net}}
and
\mathbf{B}_{\textup{var}}
defined in the main text, respectively. Meanwhile, we assume that two key conditions in (
B.14
) and (
B.15
) holds, which are verified in the last step.

In
Steps 3
and
Steps 4
, we prove that under the condition (
B.14
) and (
B.15
) i.e. the current estimation after
j
-th iteration lies in a small neighborhood of true values, the error after one step of iteration,
\mathrm{dist}^{2}_{(j+1)}
, is less than
\mathrm{dist}^{2}_{(j)}
, except for a term representing statistical error.

Specifically, in
Step 3
, we upper bound
\mathrm{dist}^{2}_{(j+1)}
by deriving upper bounds piecewise with respect to
\beta_{\textup{A}}
,
\beta_{\textup{N}}
,
\mathbf{U}
, and
\mathbf{V}
. For
\beta_{\textup{A}}^{(j+1)}
, we use the relationship that

\beta_{\textup{A}}^{(j+1)}=\beta_{\textup{A}}^{(j)}-\eta\cdot(\|\mathbf{B}_{2}^{(j)}\|_{\text{F}}^{-2}\|\mathbf{I}_{N}\|_{\text{F}}^{-2})\cdot\nabla_{\beta_{\textup{A}}}{\mathcal{L}}_{T}^{(j)}

(B.1)

and develop an upper bound

\displaystyle\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}c_{1},c_{2}\neq 0\end{subarray}}((c_{1}c_{2})^{-1}\beta_{\textup{A}}^{(j+1)}-\beta_{\textup{A}}^{*})^{2}\cdot\|\mathbf{I}_{N}\|_{\text{F}}^{2}\cdot\|\mathbf{B}_{2}^{*}\|_{\text{F}}^{2}

(B.2)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}c_{1},c_{2}\neq 0\end{subarray}}((c_{1}c_{2})^{-1}\beta_{\textup{A}}^{(j)}-\beta_{\textup{A}}^{*})^{2}\cdot\|\mathbf{I}_{N}\|_{\text{F}}^{2}\cdot\|\mathbf{B}_{2}^{*}\|_{\text{F}}^{2}-2\eta Q_{\beta_{\textup{A}},1}^{(j)}+\eta^{2}Q_{\beta_{\textup{A}},2}^{(j)},

where
Q_{\beta_{\textup{A}},1}^{(j)}
and
Q_{\beta_{\textup{A}},2}^{(j)}
are defined in (
B.38
) and (
B.36
).
For
\mathbf{U}^{(j+1)}
, we use the relationship that

\mathbf{U}^{(j+1)}=\mathbf{U}^{(j)}-\eta\cdot\|\mathbf{B}_{1}^{(j)}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{-2}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{U}}\mathcal{L}_{T}^{(j)}\cdot(\mathbf{V}^{(j)\top}\mathbf{V}^{(j)})^{-1},

(B.3)

and develop an upper bound

\displaystyle\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{Q}\in\mathrm{GL}(r),\\[2.0pt]
c_{1}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\|(c_{1}\mathbf{U}^{(j+1)}\mathbf{Q}-\mathbf{U}^{*})\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*1/2}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\cdot\|\mathbf{B}_{1}^{*}\|_{\text{F}}^{2}

(B.4)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{Q}\in\mathrm{GL}(r),\\[2.0pt]
c_{1}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\|(c_{1}\mathbf{U}^{(j)}\mathbf{Q}-\mathbf{U}^{*})\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*1/2}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\cdot\|\mathbf{B}_{1}^{*}\|_{\text{F}}^{2}-2\eta Q_{\mathbf{U},1}^{(j)}+\eta^{2}Q_{\mathbf{U},2}^{(j)},

where
Q_{\mathbf{U},1}^{(j)}
and
Q_{\mathbf{U},2}^{(j)}
